Yeah… most of the time. It’s not just that I get sad here and there, it’s like I wake up and it’s already there before anything has even happened. This heavy, gray kind of feeling, like somebody set a weight on my chest overnight and I’m supposed to just get up and carry it around.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… there are things I used to look forward to, sure, but I haven’t really been stopping to measure whether I’m enjoying them or not. Mostly it all just feels mixed together lately, like I’m going through motions and trying to get through the day.

With the shop, for example, I still care about it in the sense that it matters to me, you know? It’s not like I can just say I don’t care. But whether I’m getting pleasure out of it… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’m so caught up in what needs fixing or what’s not going right that I don’t even know what I’m feeling about the actual work anymore.

So… maybe? I guess I’ve noticed things don’t hit the same way they used to, but I don’t know how much of that is me not enjoying them and how much is just everything feeling heavy right now.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “lost interest” sounds so clean, like there was a switch and suddenly I didn’t care about things anymore. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.

I guess what I can say is… things don’t pull me toward them the way they used to. Like, with woodworking, I still know it matters to me. I can look at a piece of wood and understand, in my head, why I used to get excited about what it could become. But lately I don’t always feel that spark. Sometimes I’ll start something and I’m more aware of the effort than anything else—what has to be done, what might go wrong, what I’m behind on—so it’s hard to tell if I’m uninterested or just worn down.

So… maybe I have, some. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. It’s more like the interest is buried under a lot of noise, and I can’t always reach it.

But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself, like, pacing around or being unable to sit still in a way that stands out. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.

I mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.

So… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.

It’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.

And I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.

So yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.

It’s been… a little over two years now. I’d put it at about two years and maybe two months.

It started around when I took on the mortgage for the studio. I remember that pretty clearly because that was kind of the point where everything started feeling heavier. Not all at once, exactly, but from around then, that gray feeling just sort of settled in and never really lifted for long.

So yeah… about two years and two months, give or take.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I don’t really know what else to do anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things going, trying to work harder, figure out payment plans, tell myself there’s some way to get the studio back on track, but honestly it feels like none of it is going to matter.\n\nIt’s like no matter how much effort I put in, I can’t see things turning around. Not the studio, not my life really. I keep looking at it from every angle, like maybe if I just do this job or take this arrangement or push a little harder, but I don’t believe it anymore. I feel like I’m just standing in front of a wall, and I’m tired of pretending I can build a door through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time. It’s not just that I get sad here and there, it’s like I wake up and it’s already there before anything has even happened. This heavy, gray kind of feeling, like somebody set a weight on my chest overnight and I’m supposed to just get up and carry it around.\n\nEven when I’m in the shop, which used to be the place where I could sort of lose myself—just the smell of the wood, the tools, figuring out a cut or a joint—it all feels flat now. I’ll be standing there surrounded by things I used to care about, and it’s like there’s no color in it. Sometimes I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because I can’t get myself to feel connected to any of it. So… yeah. Sad, empty, whatever word fits. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… there are things I used to look forward to, sure, but I haven’t really been stopping to measure whether I’m enjoying them or not. Mostly it all just feels mixed together lately, like I’m going through motions and trying to get through the day.\n\nWith the shop, for example, I still care about it in the sense that it matters to me, you know? It’s not like I can just say I don’t care. But whether I’m getting pleasure out of it… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’m so caught up in what needs fixing or what’s not going right that I don’t even know what I’m feeling about the actual work anymore.\n\nSo… maybe? I guess I’ve noticed things don’t hit the same way they used to, but I don’t know how much of that is me not enjoying them and how much is just everything feeling heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “lost interest” sounds so clean, like there was a switch and suddenly I didn’t care about things anymore. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nI guess what I can say is… things don’t pull me toward them the way they used to. Like, with woodworking, I still know it matters to me. I can look at a piece of wood and understand, in my head, why I used to get excited about what it could become. But lately I don’t always feel that spark. Sometimes I’ll start something and I’m more aware of the effort than anything else—what has to be done, what might go wrong, what I’m behind on—so it’s hard to tell if I’m uninterested or just worn down.\n\nSo… maybe I have, some. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. It’s more like the interest is buried under a lot of noise, and I can’t always reach it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. About two years and two months now, I guess. That was when it kind of settled in, and since then it hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. There are things I do that I know I used to enjoy. Being in the shop, for example. I mean, wood used to be the thing that could pull me in — the smell of it, picking through boards, seeing the grain, figuring out how a piece was going to come together. That used to give me something. Not always excitement exactly, but satisfaction. A little spark, I guess.

Lately, though, most days I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. And then I get to the shop, and I’m surrounded by all the stuff I used to love, and it just feels… flat. Like I’m looking at it through dirty glass or something. I’ll stand there with a board in front of me, tools right there, and instead of feeling interested or proud or even calm, I have to fight not to just stand there staring.

So, recently? I don’t know. Maybe there are moments where something is less bad, or I get through a task and feel a little relief that it’s done. But actual pleasure or happiness… I’m having a hard time coming up with that.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I don’t really know what else to do anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things going, trying to work harder, figure out payment plans, tell myself there’s some way to get the studio back on track, but honestly it feels like none of it is going to matter.\n\nIt’s like no matter how much effort I put in, I can’t see things turning around. Not the studio, not my life really. I keep looking at it from every angle, like maybe if I just do this job or take this arrangement or push a little harder, but I don’t believe it anymore. I feel like I’m just standing in front of a wall, and I’m tired of pretending I can build a door through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time. It’s not just that I get sad here and there, it’s like I wake up and it’s already there before anything has even happened. This heavy, gray kind of feeling, like somebody set a weight on my chest overnight and I’m supposed to just get up and carry it around.\n\nEven when I’m in the shop, which used to be the place where I could sort of lose myself—just the smell of the wood, the tools, figuring out a cut or a joint—it all feels flat now. I’ll be standing there surrounded by things I used to care about, and it’s like there’s no color in it. Sometimes I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because I can’t get myself to feel connected to any of it. So… yeah. Sad, empty, whatever word fits. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… there are things I used to look forward to, sure, but I haven’t really been stopping to measure whether I’m enjoying them or not. Mostly it all just feels mixed together lately, like I’m going through motions and trying to get through the day.\n\nWith the shop, for example, I still care about it in the sense that it matters to me, you know? It’s not like I can just say I don’t care. But whether I’m getting pleasure out of it… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’m so caught up in what needs fixing or what’s not going right that I don’t even know what I’m feeling about the actual work anymore.\n\nSo… maybe? I guess I’ve noticed things don’t hit the same way they used to, but I don’t know how much of that is me not enjoying them and how much is just everything feeling heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “lost interest” sounds so clean, like there was a switch and suddenly I didn’t care about things anymore. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nI guess what I can say is… things don’t pull me toward them the way they used to. Like, with woodworking, I still know it matters to me. I can look at a piece of wood and understand, in my head, why I used to get excited about what it could become. But lately I don’t always feel that spark. Sometimes I’ll start something and I’m more aware of the effort than anything else—what has to be done, what might go wrong, what I’m behind on—so it’s hard to tell if I’m uninterested or just worn down.\n\nSo… maybe I have, some. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. It’s more like the interest is buried under a lot of noise, and I can’t always reach it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. I mean, I don’t really weigh myself regularly, so I might not be the best judge. My clothes seem to fit about the same, I guess. Maybe some days I feel like I look a little different, but that might just be how I’m seeing myself in the mirror more than an actual change.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t noticed a big weight change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself, like, pacing around or being unable to sit still in a way that stands out. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.\n\nI mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.\n\nAnd I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. I’d put it at about two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt started around when I took on the mortgage for the studio. I remember that pretty clearly because that was kind of the point where everything started feeling heavier. Not all at once, exactly, but from around then, that gray feeling just sort of settled in and never really lifted for long.\n\nSo yeah… about two years and two months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, there have been days here and there where I wasn’t quite as weighed down, or maybe I was distracted enough by work that I didn’t sit with it as much, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of a couple months where I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since I took on the studio mortgage, a little over two years ago. About two years and two months now, I guess. That was when it kind of settled in, and since then it hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way. Like, it might ease up for a day or a weekend, but not for months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything recently that you've done and actually enjoyed, where you felt some pleasure or happiness?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. There are things I do that I know I used to enjoy. Being in the shop, for example. I mean, wood used to be the thing that could pull me in — the smell of it, picking through boards, seeing the grain, figuring out how a piece was going to come together. That used to give me something. Not always excitement exactly, but satisfaction. A little spark, I guess.\n\nLately, though, most days I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. I’ll stand there with a board in front of me, tools right there, and instead of feeling interested or proud or even calm, I have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nSo, recently? Like, it might ease up for a day or a weekend, but not for months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything recently that you've done and actually enjoyed, where you felt some pleasure or happiness?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.

I mean, I don’t sit there with some clear plan in my head, if that’s what you’re asking. But there are times where I just feel so tired of being in my own life that the idea of not having to wake up and do it all again… it crosses my mind. Not in a dramatic way, more like this quiet thought of, “What if I could just not be here for a while?” Or disappear somewhere nobody needed anything from me.

It’s hard to say out loud because part of me feels like if I say it, it sounds worse than how I mean it. But yes, I guess I have had thoughts like wishing I could just vanish, or go to sleep and not have to deal with everything. I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to live exactly, but it’s… close enough that I probably shouldn’t pretend it isn’t there.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I don’t really know what else to do anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things going, trying to work harder, figure out payment plans, tell myself there’s some way to get the studio back on track, but honestly it feels like none of it is going to matter.\n\nIt’s like no matter how much effort I put in, I can’t see things turning around. Not the studio, not my life really. I keep looking at it from every angle, like maybe if I just do this job or take this arrangement or push a little harder, but I don’t believe it anymore. I feel like I’m just standing in front of a wall, and I’m tired of pretending I can build a door through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time. It’s not just that I get sad here and there, it’s like I wake up and it’s already there before anything has even happened. This heavy, gray kind of feeling, like somebody set a weight on my chest overnight and I’m supposed to just get up and carry it around.\n\nEven when I’m in the shop, which used to be the place where I could sort of lose myself—just the smell of the wood, the tools, figuring out a cut or a joint—it all feels flat now. I’ll be standing there surrounded by things I used to care about, and it’s like there’s no color in it. Sometimes I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because I can’t get myself to feel connected to any of it. So… yeah. Sad, empty, whatever word fits. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… there are things I used to look forward to, sure, but I haven’t really been stopping to measure whether I’m enjoying them or not. Mostly it all just feels mixed together lately, like I’m going through motions and trying to get through the day.\n\nWith the shop, for example, I still care about it in the sense that it matters to me, you know? It’s not like I can just say I don’t care. But whether I’m getting pleasure out of it… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’m so caught up in what needs fixing or what’s not going right that I don’t even know what I’m feeling about the actual work anymore.\n\nSo… maybe? I guess I’ve noticed things don’t hit the same way they used to, but I don’t know how much of that is me not enjoying them and how much is just everything feeling heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “lost interest” sounds so clean, like there was a switch and suddenly I didn’t care about things anymore. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nI guess what I can say is… things don’t pull me toward them the way they used to. Like, with woodworking, I still know it matters to me. I can look at a piece of wood and understand, in my head, why I used to get excited about what it could become. But lately I don’t always feel that spark. Sometimes I’ll start something and I’m more aware of the effort than anything else—what has to be done, what might go wrong, what I’m behind on—so it’s hard to tell if I’m uninterested or just worn down.\n\nSo… maybe I have, some. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. It’s more like the interest is buried under a lot of noise, and I can’t always reach it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. I mean, I don’t really weigh myself regularly, so I might not be the best judge. My clothes seem to fit about the same, I guess. Maybe some days I feel like I look a little different, but that might just be how I’m seeing myself in the mirror more than an actual change.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t noticed a big weight change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself, like, pacing around or being unable to sit still in a way that stands out. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.\n\nI mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.\n\nAnd I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe some days I feel like I look a little different, but that might just be how I’m seeing myself in the mirror more than an actual change.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t noticed a big weight change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself, like, pacing around or being unable to sit still in a way that stands out. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.\n\nI mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.\n\nAnd I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. I’d put it at about two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt started around when I took on the mortgage for the studio. I remember that pretty clearly because that was kind of the point where everything started feeling heavier. Not all at once, exactly, but from around then, that gray feeling just sort of settled in and never really lifted for long.\n\nSo yeah… about two years and two months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, there have been days here and there where I wasn’t quite as weighed down, or maybe I was distracted enough by work that I didn’t sit with it as much, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of a couple months where I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since I took on the studio mortgage, a little over two years ago. About two years and two months now, I guess. That was when it kind of settled in, and since then it hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way. Like, it might ease up for a day or a weekend, but not for months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything recently that you've done and actually enjoyed, where you felt some pleasure or happiness?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. There are things I do that I know I used to enjoy. Being in the shop, for example. I mean, wood used to be the thing that could pull me in — the smell of it, picking through boards, seeing the grain, figuring out how a piece was going to come together. That used to give me something. Not always excitement exactly, but satisfaction. A little spark, I guess.\n\nLately, though, most days I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. And then I get to the shop, and I’m surrounded by all the stuff I used to love, and it just feels… flat. Like I’m looking at it through dirty glass or something. I’ll stand there with a board in front of me, tools right there, and instead of feeling interested or proud or even calm, I have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nSo, recently? But there are times where I just feel so tired of being in my own life that the idea of not having to wake up and do it all again… it crosses my mind. Not in a dramatic way, more like this quiet thought of, “What if I could just not be here for a while?” Or disappear somewhere nobody needed anything from me.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud because part of me feels like if I say it, it sounds worse than how I mean it. But yes, I guess I have had thoughts like wishing I could just vanish, or go to sleep and not have to deal with everything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wish I had a better answer, but… not really. I keep trying to think of something small, because I know it doesn’t have to be some big enjoyable thing, but even the small stuff feels kind of blank lately.

Like, normally the shop would be the obvious answer. There used to be mornings where I’d at least look forward to getting my hands on a piece of wood, or seeing how the grain came out after a cut, or just having that quiet stretch where it was me and the tools and the work. But most days now I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there before I’ve even started the day. By the time I get to the shop, I’m surrounded by all the things that used to mean something to me, and it just feels flat. I’ll stand there looking at wood I know I should care about, and I have to push myself just to move instead of staring at it.

So in the past few weeks… I can’t really say I’ve looked forward to much. Maybe I’ve looked forward to being done with something, or to getting through the day, but that’s not the same as looking forward to an activity. It’s more like waiting for things to be over.

By the time I get to the shop, I’m surrounded by all the things that used to mean something to me, and it just feels flat. I know I keep circling back to the same answer, but… no, not really. I’m trying to be fair about it, because maybe there was some tiny second where something felt okay and I’m just not giving it credit, but nothing stands out as pleasure.

Most days I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there, like it got up before I did and was waiting. And then I’ll go into the shop, where you’d think if anything was going to help, it would be that. I mean, I’m surrounded by wood and tools and the kind of work that used to hold my attention. I used to be able to lose myself in it a little.

But lately it all just feels flat. I’ll be standing there with a board in front of me, and I know I used to care about the grain or the cut or how the piece would come together, but I don’t feel it. It’s like I’m going through the motions, and sometimes I have to fight just to not stand there staring at it. So if there’s enjoyment, it’s so small I can’t really catch it. It’s more like I get through something, not that I enjoy it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I don’t really know what else to do anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things going, trying to work harder, figure out payment plans, tell myself there’s some way to get the studio back on track, but honestly it feels like none of it is going to matter.\n\nIt’s like no matter how much effort I put in, I can’t see things turning around. Not the studio, not my life really. I keep looking at it from every angle, like maybe if I just do this job or take this arrangement or push a little harder, but I don’t believe it anymore. I feel like I’m just standing in front of a wall, and I’m tired of pretending I can build a door through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time. It’s not just that I get sad here and there, it’s like I wake up and it’s already there before anything has even happened. This heavy, gray kind of feeling, like somebody set a weight on my chest overnight and I’m supposed to just get up and carry it around.\n\nEven when I’m in the shop, which used to be the place where I could sort of lose myself—just the smell of the wood, the tools, figuring out a cut or a joint—it all feels flat now. I’ll be standing there surrounded by things I used to care about, and it’s like there’s no color in it. Sometimes I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because I can’t get myself to feel connected to any of it. So… yeah. Sad, empty, whatever word fits. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… there are things I used to look forward to, sure, but I haven’t really been stopping to measure whether I’m enjoying them or not. Mostly it all just feels mixed together lately, like I’m going through motions and trying to get through the day.\n\nWith the shop, for example, I still care about it in the sense that it matters to me, you know? It’s not like I can just say I don’t care. But whether I’m getting pleasure out of it… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’m so caught up in what needs fixing or what’s not going right that I don’t even know what I’m feeling about the actual work anymore.\n\nSo… maybe? I guess I’ve noticed things don’t hit the same way they used to, but I don’t know how much of that is me not enjoying them and how much is just everything feeling heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “lost interest” sounds so clean, like there was a switch and suddenly I didn’t care about things anymore. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nI guess what I can say is… things don’t pull me toward them the way they used to. Like, with woodworking, I still know it matters to me. I can look at a piece of wood and understand, in my head, why I used to get excited about what it could become. But lately I don’t always feel that spark. Sometimes I’ll start something and I’m more aware of the effort than anything else—what has to be done, what might go wrong, what I’m behind on—so it’s hard to tell if I’m uninterested or just worn down.\n\nSo… maybe I have, some. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. It’s more like the interest is buried under a lot of noise, and I can’t always reach it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. I mean, I don’t really weigh myself regularly, so I might not be the best judge. My clothes seem to fit about the same, I guess. Maybe some days I feel like I look a little different, but that might just be how I’m seeing myself in the mirror more than an actual change.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t noticed a big weight change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself, like, pacing around or being unable to sit still in a way that stands out. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.\n\nI mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.\n\nAnd I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. I’d put it at about two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt started around when I took on the mortgage for the studio. I remember that pretty clearly because that was kind of the point where everything started feeling heavier. Not all at once, exactly, but from around then, that gray feeling just sort of settled in and never really lifted for long.\n\nSo yeah… about two years and two months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, there have been days here and there where I wasn’t quite as weighed down, or maybe I was distracted enough by work that I didn’t sit with it as much, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of a couple months where I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since I took on the studio mortgage, a little over two years ago. About two years and two months now, I guess. That was when it kind of settled in, and since then it hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way. Like, it might ease up for a day or a weekend, but not for months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything recently that you've done and actually enjoyed, where you felt some pleasure or happiness?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. There are things I do that I know I used to enjoy. Being in the shop, for example. I mean, wood used to be the thing that could pull me in — the smell of it, picking through boards, seeing the grain, figuring out how a piece was going to come together. That used to give me something. Not always excitement exactly, but satisfaction. A little spark, I guess.\n\nLately, though, most days I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. And then I get to the shop, and I’m surrounded by all the stuff I used to love, and it just feels… flat. Like I’m looking at it through dirty glass or something. I’ll stand there with a board in front of me, tools right there, and instead of feeling interested or proud or even calm, I have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nSo, recently? I don’t know. Maybe there are moments where something is less bad, or I get through a task and feel a little relief that it’s done. But actual pleasure or happiness… I’m having a hard time coming up with that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I don’t sit there with some clear plan in my head, if that’s what you’re asking. But there are times where I just feel so tired of being in my own life that the idea of not having to wake up and do it all again… it crosses my mind. Not in a dramatic way, more like this quiet thought of, “What if I could just not be here for a while?” Or disappear somewhere nobody needed anything from me.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud because part of me feels like if I say it, it sounds worse than how I mean it. But yes, I guess I have had thoughts like wishing I could just vanish, or go to sleep and not have to deal with everything. I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to live exactly, but it’s… close enough that I probably shouldn’t pretend it isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity, no matter how small, that you have looked forward to in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I wish I had a better answer, but… not really. I keep trying to think of something small, because I know it doesn’t have to be some big enjoyable thing, but even the small stuff feels kind of blank lately.\n\nLike, normally the shop would be the obvious answer. There used to be mornings where I’d at least look forward to getting my hands on a piece of wood, or seeing how the grain came out after a cut, or just having that quiet stretch where it was me and the tools and the work. But most days now I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there before I’ve even started the day. By the time I get to the shop, I’m surrounded by all the things that used to mean something to me, and it just feels flat. I’ll stand there looking at wood I know I should care about, and I have to push myself just to move instead of staring at it.\n\nSo in the past few weeks… I can’t really say I’ve looked forward to much. Maybe I’ve looked forward to being done with something, or to getting through the day, but that’s not the same as looking forward to an activity. It’s more like waiting for things to be over.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any recent situation where you felt a sense of pleasure or enjoyment, no matter how small it might seem?\n\nPatient: I know I keep circling back to the same answer, but… no, not really. I’m trying to be fair about it, because maybe there was some tiny second where something felt okay and I’m just not giving it credit, but nothing stands out as pleasure.\n\nMost days I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there, like it got up before I did and was waiting. And then I’ll go into the shop, where you’d think if anything was going to help, it would be that. I mean, I’m surrounded by wood and tools and the kind of work that used to hold my attention. I used to be able to lose myself in it a little.\n\nBut lately it all just feels flat. I’ll be standing there with a board in front of me, and I know I used to care about the grain or the cut or how the piece would come together, but I don’t feel it. It’s like I’m going through the motions, and sometimes I have to fight just to not stand there staring at it. So if there’s enjoyment, it’s so small I can’t really catch it. It’s more like I get through something, not that I enjoy it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It’s more like waiting for things to be over.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any recent situation where you felt a sense of pleasure or enjoyment, no matter how small it might seem?\n\nPatient: I know I keep circling back to the same answer, but… no, not really. I’m trying to be fair about it, because maybe there was some tiny second where something felt okay and I’m just not giving it credit, but nothing stands out as pleasure.\n\nMost days I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there, like it got up before I did and was waiting. And then I’ll go into the shop, where you’d think if anything was going to help, it would be that. I mean, I’m surrounded by wood and tools and the kind of work that used to hold my attention. I used to be able to lose myself in it a little.\n\nBut lately it all just feels flat. I’ll be standing there with a board in front of me, and I know I used to care about the grain or the cut or how the piece would come together, but I don’t feel it. It’s like I’m going through the motions, and sometimes I have to fight just to not stand there staring at it. So if there’s enjoyment, it’s so small I can’t really catch it. It’s more like I get through something, not that I enjoy it.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you remember the last time you felt genuine pleasure or interest in something?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… I’m having a hard time remembering one. I keep trying to go back through the last stretch of days or weeks and find something I can point to, like, “There, that was it,” but it all kind of has the same color to it, if that makes sense.

Most days I wake up and it’s like this heavy, gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. I don’t wake up neutral and then get dragged down by the day — it’s already there. And that makes it hard to tell when the last real pleasure was, because even things that used to get through to me don’t really seem to anymore.

The shop used to be that place for me. I mean, working with wood wasn’t just a job in my head. There were parts of it I genuinely cared about — the smell of it, the feel of a good board, seeing the grain come out, figuring out how a piece was going to fit together. I used to get interested without even trying. But now I can be standing right there, surrounded by all of it, and it just feels flat. Like I know I’m supposed to care, and I remember caring, but I can’t reach it.

Sometimes I’ll just stand there looking at the wood or the tools, and I have to force myself to move, because otherwise I could just stay there staring. Not because I’m thinking deeply about it or enjoying it — more because nothing in me is catching on it. So… the honest answer is I don’t really remember the last time I felt genuine pleasure or interest. I remember what it used to feel like, but I don’t remember the last time I actually felt it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I don’t really know what else to do anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things going, trying to work harder, figure out payment plans, tell myself there’s some way to get the studio back on track, but honestly it feels like none of it is going to matter.\n\nIt’s like no matter how much effort I put in, I can’t see things turning around. Not the studio, not my life really. I keep looking at it from every angle, like maybe if I just do this job or take this arrangement or push a little harder, but I don’t believe it anymore. I feel like I’m just standing in front of a wall, and I’m tired of pretending I can build a door through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time. It’s not just that I get sad here and there, it’s like I wake up and it’s already there before anything has even happened. This heavy, gray kind of feeling, like somebody set a weight on my chest overnight and I’m supposed to just get up and carry it around.\n\nEven when I’m in the shop, which used to be the place where I could sort of lose myself—just the smell of the wood, the tools, figuring out a cut or a joint—it all feels flat now. I’ll be standing there surrounded by things I used to care about, and it’s like there’s no color in it. Sometimes I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because I can’t get myself to feel connected to any of it. So… yeah. Sad, empty, whatever word fits. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… there are things I used to look forward to, sure, but I haven’t really been stopping to measure whether I’m enjoying them or not. Mostly it all just feels mixed together lately, like I’m going through motions and trying to get through the day.\n\nWith the shop, for example, I still care about it in the sense that it matters to me, you know? It’s not like I can just say I don’t care. But whether I’m getting pleasure out of it… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’m so caught up in what needs fixing or what’s not going right that I don’t even know what I’m feeling about the actual work anymore.\n\nSo… maybe? I guess I’ve noticed things don’t hit the same way they used to, but I don’t know how much of that is me not enjoying them and how much is just everything feeling heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “lost interest” sounds so clean, like there was a switch and suddenly I didn’t care about things anymore. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nI guess what I can say is… things don’t pull me toward them the way they used to. Like, with woodworking, I still know it matters to me. I can look at a piece of wood and understand, in my head, why I used to get excited about what it could become. But lately I don’t always feel that spark. Sometimes I’ll start something and I’m more aware of the effort than anything else—what has to be done, what might go wrong, what I’m behind on—so it’s hard to tell if I’m uninterested or just worn down.\n\nSo… maybe I have, some. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. It’s more like the interest is buried under a lot of noise, and I can’t always reach it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. I mean, I don’t really weigh myself regularly, so I might not be the best judge. My clothes seem to fit about the same, I guess. Maybe some days I feel like I look a little different, but that might just be how I’m seeing myself in the mirror more than an actual change.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t noticed a big weight change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself, like, pacing around or being unable to sit still in a way that stands out. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.\n\nI mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.\n\nAnd I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. I’d put it at about two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt started around when I took on the mortgage for the studio. I remember that pretty clearly because that was kind of the point where everything started feeling heavier. Not all at once, exactly, but from around then, that gray feeling just sort of settled in and never really lifted for long.\n\nSo yeah… about two years and two months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, there have been days here and there where I wasn’t quite as weighed down, or maybe I was distracted enough by work that I didn’t sit with it as much, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of a couple months where I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since I took on the studio mortgage, a little over two years ago. About two years and two months now, I guess. That was when it kind of settled in, and since then it hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way. Like, it might ease up for a day or a weekend, but not for months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything recently that you've done and actually enjoyed, where you felt some pleasure or happiness?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. There are things I do that I know I used to enjoy. Being in the shop, for example. I mean, wood used to be the thing that could pull me in — the smell of it, picking through boards, seeing the grain, figuring out how a piece was going to come together. That used to give me something. Not always excitement exactly, but satisfaction. A little spark, I guess.\n\nLately, though, most days I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. And then I get to the shop, and I’m surrounded by all the stuff I used to love, and it just feels… flat. Like I’m looking at it through dirty glass or something. I’ll stand there with a board in front of me, tools right there, and instead of feeling interested or proud or even calm, I have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nSo, recently? I don’t know. Maybe there are moments where something is less bad, or I get through a task and feel a little relief that it’s done. But actual pleasure or happiness… I’m having a hard time coming up with that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I don’t sit there with some clear plan in my head, if that’s what you’re asking. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.\n\nI mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.\n\nAnd I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. I’d put it at about two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt started around when I took on the mortgage for the studio. I remember that pretty clearly because that was kind of the point where everything started feeling heavier. Not all at once, exactly, but from around then, that gray feeling just sort of settled in and never really lifted for long.\n\nSo yeah… about two years and two months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, there have been days here and there where I wasn’t quite as weighed down, or maybe I was distracted enough by work that I didn’t sit with it as much, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of a couple months where I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since I took on the studio mortgage, a little over two years ago. About two years and two months now, I guess. That was when it kind of settled in, and since then it hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way. Like, it might ease up for a day or a weekend, but not for months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything recently that you've done and actually enjoyed, where you felt some pleasure or happiness?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. There are things I do that I know I used to enjoy. Being in the shop, for example. I mean, wood used to be the thing that could pull me in — the smell of it, picking through boards, seeing the grain, figuring out how a piece was going to come together. That used to give me something. Not always excitement exactly, but satisfaction. A little spark, I guess.\n\nLately, though, most days I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. And then I get to the shop, and I’m surrounded by all the stuff I used to love, and it just feels… flat. Like I’m looking at it through dirty glass or something. I’ll stand there with a board in front of me, tools right there, and instead of feeling interested or proud or even calm, I have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nSo, recently? I don’t know. Maybe there are moments where something is less bad, or I get through a task and feel a little relief that it’s done. But actual pleasure or happiness… I’m having a hard time coming up with that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I don’t sit there with some clear plan in my head, if that’s what you’re asking. But there are times where I just feel so tired of being in my own life that the idea of not having to wake up and do it all again… it crosses my mind. Not in a dramatic way, more like this quiet thought of, “What if I could just not be here for a while?” Or disappear somewhere nobody needed anything from me.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud because part of me feels like if I say it, it sounds worse than how I mean it. But yes, I guess I have had thoughts like wishing I could just vanish, or go to sleep and not have to deal with everything. I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to live exactly, but it’s… close enough that I probably shouldn’t pretend it isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity, no matter how small, that you have looked forward to in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I wish I had a better answer, but… not really. I keep trying to think of something small, because I know it doesn’t have to be some big enjoyable thing, but even the small stuff feels kind of blank lately.\n\nLike, normally the shop would be the obvious answer. There used to be mornings where I’d at least look forward to getting my hands on a piece of wood, or seeing how the grain came out after a cut, or just having that quiet stretch where it was me and the tools and the work. But most days now I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there before I’ve even started the day. By the time I get to the shop, I’m surrounded by all the things that used to mean something to me, and it just feels flat. I’ll stand there looking at wood I know I should care about, and I have to push myself just to move instead of staring at it.\n\nSo in the past few weeks… I can’t really say I’ve looked forward to much. Maybe I’ve looked forward to being done with something, or to getting through the day, but that’s not the same as looking forward to an activity. It’s more like waiting for things to be over.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any recent situation where you felt a sense of pleasure or enjoyment, no matter how small it might seem?\n\nPatient: I know I keep circling back to the same answer, but… no, not really. I’m trying to be fair about it, because maybe there was some tiny second where something felt okay and I’m just not giving it credit, but nothing stands out as pleasure.\n\nMost days I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there, like it got up before I did and was waiting. And then I’ll go into the shop, where you’d think if anything was going to help, it would be that. I mean, I’m surrounded by wood and tools and the kind of work that used to hold my attention. I used to be able to lose myself in it a little.\n\nBut lately it all just feels flat. I’ll be standing there with a board in front of me, and I know I used to care about the grain or the cut or how the piece would come together, but I don’t feel it. It’s like I’m going through the motions, and sometimes I have to fight just to not stand there staring at it. So if there’s enjoyment, it’s so small I can’t really catch it. It’s more like I get through something, not that I enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you felt genuine pleasure or interest in something?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m having a hard time remembering one. I keep trying to go back through the last stretch of days or weeks and find something I can point to, like, “There, that was it,” but it all kind of has the same color to it, if that makes sense.\n\nMost days I wake up and it’s like this heavy, gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. I don’t wake up neutral and then get dragged down by the day — it’s already there. And that makes it hard to tell when the last real pleasure was, because even things that used to get through to me don’t really seem to anymore.\n\nThe shop used to be that place for me. I mean, working with wood wasn’t just a job in my head. There were parts of it I genuinely cared about — the smell of it, the feel of a good board, seeing the grain come out, figuring out how a piece was going to fit together. I used to get interested without even trying. But now I can be standing right there, surrounded by all of it, and it just feels flat. Like I know I’m supposed to care, and I remember caring, but I can’t reach it.\n\nSometimes I’ll just stand there looking at the wood or the tools, and I have to force myself to move, because otherwise I could just stay there staring. Not because I’m thinking deeply about it or enjoying it — more because nothing in me is catching on it. I remember what it used to feel like, but I don’t remember the last time I actually felt it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really, no. I know I probably sound like I’m repeating myself, but I keep looking for something I can honestly say was enjoyable, and I just… I can’t find much.

But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve completely lost interest. It’s more like the interest is buried under a lot of noise, and I can’t always reach it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. I mean, I don’t really weigh myself regularly, so I might not be the best judge. My clothes seem to fit about the same, I guess. Maybe some days I feel like I look a little different, but that might just be how I’m seeing myself in the mirror more than an actual change.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t noticed a big weight change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself, like, pacing around or being unable to sit still in a way that stands out. And I don’t think I’ve been obviously moving slower either, at least not that anyone’s pointed out to me.\n\nI mean, some days I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. With work, I’m still moving around because I have to, and when I’m sitting, I’m usually just sitting there. I don’t feel especially restless, like I need to jump up every few minutes.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in any clear way. If it’s happening, I haven’t really been aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always about some big thing that happened, though. A lot of the time it’s work stuff, and I know work mistakes happen, I know that. Like if a joint doesn’t line up perfectly, or I cut something a little off and have to redo it, or I miss getting a bid in on time… in my head I don’t just go, “Okay, that was a mistake, fix it and move on.” I go straight to, “What is wrong with me?” or “Maybe I’m not actually that good at this.” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I’m a lousy carpenter, or I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place.\n\nAnd I can usually see, at least later, that that’s a pretty harsh jump. I mean, one bad joint doesn’t mean the whole house falls down, you know? But in the moment it doesn’t feel like one mistake. It feels like proof. Like I’ve been pretending I can handle things and the mistake is just showing what’s really true.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Even for things that are probably just normal problems. I have a hard time keeping them normal in my own mind.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray feeling that you described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. I’d put it at about two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt started around when I took on the mortgage for the studio. I remember that pretty clearly because that was kind of the point where everything started feeling heavier. Not all at once, exactly, but from around then, that gray feeling just sort of settled in and never really lifted for long.\n\nSo yeah… about two years and two months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, there have been days here and there where I wasn’t quite as weighed down, or maybe I was distracted enough by work that I didn’t sit with it as much, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of a couple months where I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since I took on the studio mortgage, a little over two years ago. About two years and two months now, I guess. That was when it kind of settled in, and since then it hasn’t really lifted in any lasting way. Like, it might ease up for a day or a weekend, but not for months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything recently that you've done and actually enjoyed, where you felt some pleasure or happiness?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of something, and that’s kind of the problem. There are things I do that I know I used to enjoy. Being in the shop, for example. I mean, wood used to be the thing that could pull me in — the smell of it, picking through boards, seeing the grain, figuring out how a piece was going to come together. That used to give me something. Not always excitement exactly, but satisfaction. A little spark, I guess.\n\nLately, though, most days I wake up and it’s like that heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. And then I get to the shop, and I’m surrounded by all the stuff I used to love, and it just feels… flat. Like I’m looking at it through dirty glass or something. I’ll stand there with a board in front of me, tools right there, and instead of feeling interested or proud or even calm, I have to fight not to just stand there staring.\n\nSo, recently? I don’t know. Maybe there are moments where something is less bad, or I get through a task and feel a little relief that it’s done. But actual pleasure or happiness… I’m having a hard time coming up with that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I don’t sit there with some clear plan in my head, if that’s what you’re asking. But there are times where I just feel so tired of being in my own life that the idea of not having to wake up and do it all again… it crosses my mind. Not in a dramatic way, more like this quiet thought of, “What if I could just not be here for a while?” Or disappear somewhere nobody needed anything from me.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud because part of me feels like if I say it, it sounds worse than how I mean it. But yes, I guess I have had thoughts like wishing I could just vanish, or go to sleep and not have to deal with everything. I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to live exactly, but it’s… close enough that I probably shouldn’t pretend it isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity, no matter how small, that you have looked forward to in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I wish I had a better answer, but… not really. I keep trying to think of something small, because I know it doesn’t have to be some big enjoyable thing, but even the small stuff feels kind of blank lately.\n\nLike, normally the shop would be the obvious answer. There used to be mornings where I’d at least look forward to getting my hands on a piece of wood, or seeing how the grain came out after a cut, or just having that quiet stretch where it was me and the tools and the work. But most days now I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there before I’ve even started the day. By the time I get to the shop, I’m surrounded by all the things that used to mean something to me, and it just feels flat. I’ll stand there looking at wood I know I should care about, and I have to push myself just to move instead of staring at it.\n\nSo in the past few weeks… I can’t really say I’ve looked forward to much. Maybe I’ve looked forward to being done with something, or to getting through the day, but that’s not the same as looking forward to an activity. It’s more like waiting for things to be over.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any recent situation where you felt a sense of pleasure or enjoyment, no matter how small it might seem?\n\nPatient: I know I keep circling back to the same answer, but… no, not really. I’m trying to be fair about it, because maybe there was some tiny second where something felt okay and I’m just not giving it credit, but nothing stands out as pleasure.\n\nMost days I wake up and that heavy, gray feeling is already there, like it got up before I did and was waiting. And then I’ll go into the shop, where you’d think if anything was going to help, it would be that. I mean, I’m surrounded by wood and tools and the kind of work that used to hold my attention. I used to be able to lose myself in it a little.\n\nBut lately it all just feels flat. I’ll be standing there with a board in front of me, and I know I used to care about the grain or the cut or how the piece would come together, but I don’t feel it. It’s like I’m going through the motions, and sometimes I have to fight just to not stand there staring at it. So if there’s enjoyment, it’s so small I can’t really catch it. It’s more like I get through something, not that I enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you felt genuine pleasure or interest in something?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m having a hard time remembering one. I keep trying to go back through the last stretch of days or weeks and find something I can point to, like, “There, that was it,” but it all kind of has the same color to it, if that makes sense.\n\nMost days I wake up and it’s like this heavy, gray feeling is already sitting on me before I’ve even done anything. I don’t wake up neutral and then get dragged down by the day — it’s already there. And that makes it hard to tell when the last real pleasure was, because even things that used to get through to me don’t really seem to anymore.\n\nThe shop used to be that place for me. I mean, working with wood wasn’t just a job in my head. There were parts of it I genuinely cared about — the smell of it, the feel of a good board, seeing the grain come out, figuring out how a piece was going to fit together. I used to get interested without even trying. But now I can be standing right there, surrounded by all of it, and it just feels flat. Like I know I’m supposed to care, and I remember caring, but I can’t reach it.\n\nSometimes I’ll just stand there looking at the wood or the tools, and I have to force myself to move, because otherwise I could just stay there staring. Not because I’m thinking deeply about it or enjoying it — more because nothing in me is catching on it. So… the honest answer is I don’t really remember the last time I felt genuine pleasure or interest. I remember what it used to feel like, but I don’t remember the last time I actually felt it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent activity that you found pleasurable or enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I know I probably sound like I’m repeating myself, but I keep looking for something I can honestly say was enjoyable, and I just… I can’t find much.\n\nEven the shop, which used to be the thing I could count on, doesn’t really do it lately. I’ll be in there surrounded by the wood and tools, things I used to care about without even thinking, and it all feels kind of flat. Like I know, in my head, that I used to love the work — choosing a piece, seeing the grain, getting into the rhythm of it — but lately I’m just standing there with this heavy gray feeling already on me from the minute I wake up.\n\nSometimes I’ll look at a board or a half-finished piece and I have to make myself move, because otherwise I could just stand there staring at it. Not enjoying it, not really thinking anything useful, just… stuck. So if you’re asking about something pleasurable or enjoyable recently, I don’t think I can honestly say there’s been one. At most I get through things. I don’t really enjoy them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yes. Very much so. I mean, that’s probably one of the things that bothers me the most, because it’s not like I’m just bored with something new or tired of a hobby I picked up last week. It’s the things that used to feel like mine, especially the shop.

And then I go into the shop, and I’m surrounded by wood and tools and pieces I know I used to care about. I used to notice the grain, the smell, the way a board would feel in my hands, all of that. It used to bring me something — maybe not happiness in some big dramatic way, but satisfaction, interest, a reason to keep going with it.

Now it just feels flat. I can look at the same kind of wood I used to get excited about and there’s just nothing there. Sometimes I have to actually fight not to just stand there staring at it, because I’m not drawn into it the way I used to be. It’s like I remember that it used to matter to me, but I can’t feel that anymore. So yes, I’ve definitely noticed a lack of pleasure. It’s pretty hard to miss.
